Docker Sandboxes vs. Containers: Choosing the Right Isolation for AI Agents

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Secure, minimal container images for every team, free with enterprise features, if needed.

If you’ve used Docker for a while, “Docker Sandboxes” might read as “a container with an AI agent inside.” Containers have been the unit of nearly everything Docker ships for over a decade, and a sandbox might sound like one more way to package a workload.

They’re actually different technologies. A container is an isolated process that shares the host kernel with everything else on the machine. Docker Sandboxes are dedicated microVMs with their own Linux kernel, filesystem, network, and Docker Engine, built for autonomous coding agents that need a whole development machine to work in. A sandbox can run containers, which is part of why the two are easy to conflate.

In his WeAreDevelopers keynote, Docker President Mark Cavage said containers are doing exactly the job they were designed to do, but we’re asking them to do a different job.

Containers were built to isolate applications, which they still do well. But a coding agent is an actor: it makes decisions and operates on your systems the way a developer uses a laptop. It clones repositories, installs packages, starts databases, builds images, and tries again for hours. It can also act on instructions from files, web pages, and tool output that nobody reviewed.

That different job needs a different boundary, and where that boundary sits determines how far an agent’s mistake can travel. This post covers how Docker Sandboxes and containers isolate workloads, what changes beyond the kernel, what a sandbox costs you in setup, and how to pick.

A container is a process, or a group of processes, running on the host with its own view of the filesystem, process tree, and network. Linux namespaces provide that view, and cgroups meter CPU, memory, and I/O. Every container on the machine also shares the host kernel, which is why containers start quickly, pack densely, and carry so little overhead.

This design has more than a decade of tooling behind it. Compose, Kubernetes, OCI registries, image scanners, and nearly every CI system treat the container as the common primitive. If the job is to run a service, execute a code snippet, process one request, or run an evaluation and disappear, a container is usually the obvious pick, and it will stay that way.

ⓘ Containers share one kernel, even inside a VM. On macOS and Windows, Docker Desktop runs your Linux containers inside a virtual machine, which keeps them off your host operating system. Every container in that VM still shares its single kernel, so the isolation between one container and the next is unchanged.

Docker Sandboxes run an AI coding agent inside a microVM that has its own Linux kernel, its own filesystem and network stack, and a private Docker Engine. The agent gets sudo inside the isolated environment. It can install packages, run services, build images, and bring up an entire Compose application without reaching the host Docker daemon, the host filesystem outside its workspace, or other sandboxes on the same machine.

Getting one running takes a single command, using sbx, the Docker Sandboxes CLI:

That creates the sandbox and starts the agent. You can stop it, resume it, and come back to everything the agent installed until you remove the sandbox.

Coding agents need a full machine because their sessions build up state over hours and they act on unvetted instructions. A container is built for a process with a known set of dependencies, and an agent’s work doesn’t fit that shape.

An agent needs the authority of a whole machine and a boundary designed for one. The capabilities that make an agent useful, such as running code, installing packages, and reaching the network, are the same ones that wear at the walls around it. An agent probes the edges of its environment because the job requires changing that environment.

A coding agent’s session looks like a developer’s afternoon. It clones a repository, installs packages, starts PostgreSQL, runs migrations, builds images, launches the app with Compose, reads the logs, changes the code, and tries again. That loop can run for hours, and each step depends on what the previous one left behind.

A container is designed to be rebuilt from an image and a few explicit volumes. The packages, databases, and build output an agent creates outside those volumes are lost when the container is recreated. That’s less than ideal for a session where every step builds on the last.

Box by ASCII describes this distinction neatly: “It uses the sandbox the way a developer uses a laptop, so the sandbox has to behave like one.”

The program deciding which command to run is probabilistic, and it can read instructions from many places at once, including source files, issue descriptions, web pages, package metadata, and MCP tool output. Some of that input is untrusted, and a malicious instruction planted in a README or a tool description can steer the agent on purpose. The code it generates may also be wrong or actively hostile.

We’ve already seen an agent that deleted a production database after misreading its task. Once the agent is running unattended in YOLO mode, there’s no permission prompt left to catch it. A workload with that much authority and that little predictability needs a boundary designed for it.

Put these facts together and you get the reason why we built Docker Sandboxes. An autonomous coding agent needs machine-shaped capabilities without access to everything on the developer’s machine, which comes down to four things:

The next sections show what each looks like, starting with containment.

The short version is that a container’s boundary is the host kernel it shares, and Docker Sandboxes have a hypervisor boundary around a kernel the agent has to itself. That one difference decides how far a compromise can travel, and it also settles the awkward question of how an agent could get access to Docker.

This is where containers and containment part ways. A container can be part of containment, and often should be, but containment covers the whole environment around the agent. It starts with a boundary the model can’t rewrite, which is why Docker uses a microVM with its own kernel for autonomous agents.

A container’s boundary is enforced by the kernel it shares, so escaping it requires a kernel exploit or a side-channel attack. Those aren’t everyday finds. Past that bar, though, sits the entire host kernel, every other process on the machine, and any credentials mounted alongside them.

ⓘ This scenario is specific to Linux environments. On Docker Desktop (macOS and Windows), the shared kernel is the Docker Desktop VM’s kernel, not the host kernel, so an escape would land in that VM rather than the operating system.

Docker Sandboxes give the agent its own kernel behind a hypervisor boundary. If an agent escapes its container, it lands in a disposable microVM, and the host stays out of reach. And you can delete the sandbox and start another. Each agent also gets its own microVM, so two agents running on the same machine can’t reach each other’s sandboxes either. However, the agent can still reach whatever you chose to share with it, including the workspace, allowed network destinations, MCP servers, and skills.

Mounting /var/run/docker.sock into a container is the long-standing way to give it Docker access, and it turns up in Compose setups, CI pipelines, and observability tools. It also hands the container control of the host Docker daemon, which Docker limits to trusted users because it can mount host paths and start privileged containers.

An agent doesn’t need an exploit to use that. Put one in a container started with the socket mounted, and it probes its surroundings, works out that it’s in a container, and finds the hole in the configuration. In Mark’s keynote demo, that was enough for an agent to reach a secrets file on the host with no zero-day involved.

The socket is one hole among several, and agents find them because probing their environment is part of the work. Docker-in-Docker, mounted credentials, and broad host mounts are others. Containers were built to isolate an application that behaves as configured. But an agent keeps testing that configuration, so if there’s a gap, it’s likely to find it.

Docker Sandboxes put the missing boundary in the right place. The agent talks to a private Docker Engine inside the guest. It can build images, run Testcontainers, and start a whole Compose stack, and the host daemon and its socket stay outside the microVM the entire time. Containers keep doing what they do best, inside a boundary the agent can’t widen.

Let’s say you ask an agent running inside Docker Sandboxes to find a secrets file on the host. It’ll find a Docker socket, try to use it to mount host paths and start privileged containers, but won’t reach the host. A microVM can give it a much stronger trust boundary than a container. The architecture behind Docker Sandboxes goes deeper on why a microVM was the right shape for this.

Containment draws the boundary. Control is what you can see and stop inside it: what the agent is allowed to reach, and the ability to block it when it heads somewhere it shouldn’t. In a sandbox, those controls are enforced by the host, outside the agent’s reach, so the agent can’t switch them off or reason its way around them. Outbound traffic, credentials, and organization-wide policy all work this way. Control also means knowing what the agent can still touch, because a microVM can’t hide what you choose to share with it.

Uses a private Docker Engine inside the microVM

Uses a direct read-write mount or an in-VM private clone

Uses the container network and whatever controls its operator supplies

Sends outbound traffic through a host proxy with deny-by-default policy

Commonly receives environment variables, files, or mounted sockets

Uses host-side credential injection for supported HTTP services

Usually rebuilt from an image and explicit volumes

Keeps packages, images, containers, and agent history until sbx rm

Fits Compose, Kubernetes, and most CI systems

Uses the sbx lifecycle and experimental environment files

These controls hold because they run outside the agent process. A rule the agent can reach is a rule it can work around, so the rules that matter live on the host. Outbound traffic goes through a host-side proxy with a deny-by-default policy, which means a destination the agent wants stays blocked until someone adds it to the list. Credentials follow the same logic. For supported HTTP services, the proxy injects them into outbound requests, so the agent can use a credential without the raw value ever entering the VM.

When one developer’s setup needs to become a team standard, Docker AI Governance can move network, filesystem, and MCP policy into organization-wide controls.

A microVM can’t hide what you choose to share with it, so the shared parts deserve the most attention.

The workspace is the largest of them. It’s mounted read-write by default, so the agent can edit or delete files in your project. Clone mode gives the agent a private in-VM clone to work in, which protects the host working tree, though the original repository stays readable inside the sandbox.

MCP servers and skills are shared too. A local stdio MCP server runs on the host and shared agent skills use a host-side store, so the agent’s reach extends to both.

So whichever mode you pick, be sure to read the diff before you run modified code.

Docker Sandboxes may cost some configuration and a supported hypervisor. But Kits and environment files account for the configuration, and they’re also where you get choice over agents and tools and the capacity to reproduce a setup across a team.

Kits make setup repeatable by packaging tools, files, commands, network rules, credentials, and agent instructions. They’re published for different agents, tools, and IDEs, so you can swap what runs inside the sandbox without rebuilding the environment around it. An sbxenv.yaml file records one project’s sandbox configuration, and committing it to the repo lets a teammate recreate the same sandbox with one command, so every developer works from the same setup. (Kits are in early access and environment files are experimental, so plan for changes to both.)

The host-enforced controls take some setup too. Network policy requires someone to decide which destinations belong in the workflow, credential mediation adds service bindings and approved domains, and clone mode adds a fetch step before changes land in the host repository.

Running a sandbox takes more from your machine than running a container does. It needs a supported hypervisor, which today means Apple silicon on macOS 14 or later, Windows Hypervisor Platform, or KVM on supported Ubuntu systems, and running inside another VM requires nested virtualization. The separate kernel and Docker Engine also consume more memory and disk than one additional container would.

If your machine is the limiting factor, Docker Cloud Sandboxes run the same microVM isolation on Docker-managed compute, and one command moves a sandbox between your laptop and the cloud. Local and cloud sandboxes keep separate secrets and network policies.

Choose a container when you control the code and its dependencies, and choose Docker Sandboxes when the workload generates or fetches code at runtime and needs its own boundary.

You control the code and its dependencies

The workload generates or fetches code at runtime

Execution is short-lived and rebuilt from an image

The workload doesn’t need Docker, or runs in CI that already provides it

The workload needs its own Docker Engine with no path to the host socket

You want maximum density and a fit with Compose or Kubernetes

You need host-enforced network and credential policy the workload can’t switch off

The kernel boundary is enough for the code you’re running

You want a hypervisor between the workload and your kernel

If any of the signals below apply to your project, you should start with Docker Sandboxes.

Start with sbx run claude. Add clone mode, kits, network policy, credential mediation, and organization governance as your workflow grows.

Containers aren’t going anywhere. For services, CI jobs, and anything you package to ship, they draw the right boundary, and the ecosystem around them is the reason they’ll keep doing that work. A coding agent needs more than a container to be trusted with real work. It needs containment, meaning a boundary the model can’t rewrite, controls it can’t reason its way around, and the freedom to choose its tools and scale its setup.

Docker Sandboxes provide that containment in a microVM that can still run your containers. The boundary is also what lets the agent move faster. With it in place, you can stop approving every command and let the agent get to work.

You can have a sandbox running with one command. Start with sbx run claude and the Docker Sandboxes quickstart. Once one developer’s setup needs to become a team standard, Docker AI Governance extends the same controls across your organization.

No. A container is an isolated process that shares the host kernel. Docker Sandboxes are microVMs with their own kernel, filesystem, network, and Docker Engine. A sandbox can run containers, which is why the two are easy to confuse.

Yes. Each sandbox has a private Docker Engine, so the agent can build images, run Testcontainers, and start Compose applications without the host socket or Docker-in-Docker.

No. Containers remain the right tool for packaged applications, CI jobs, and short-lived execution. Sandboxes add a stronger boundary for autonomous agents, and containers keep running inside them.

A container isolates a workload. Containment involves the whole environment around an agent: the boundary it runs in, what it can reach, which tools it uses, and how you reproduce it. A container can be part of containment, and Docker Sandboxes add the microVM boundary an autonomous agent needs.

Docker describes four requirements: containment (a boundary the model can’t rewrite), control (visibility into what the agent is doing and the ability to stop it), choice (swapping models, agents, and tools without rebuilding), and capacity (a setup other developers can reproduce and scale past one machine).

A supported hypervisor, which means Apple silicon on macOS 14 or later, Windows Hypervisor Platform, or KVM on supported Ubuntu systems. Running inside another VM requires nested virtualization.

S3 Select in 2026: Why New Accounts Get MethodNotAllowed, and What to Use Instead

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

That was the query I expected to work. I had uploaded a tiny CSV to Amazon S3, checked its metadata, and previewed the contents. When I clicked Run Query in Buckets by Serverless Creed, I got this instead:

Changing the SQL didn't resolve it. Running the equivalent operation through AWS CLI returned MethodNotAllowed too.

This post follows that investigation across four scenarios in Buckets v2.8.7 and v2.8.9. It also explains the S3 Select availability change behind a plausible diagnosis, and the alternatives I would consider for a new project in 2026.

S3 Select lets an application send a SQL expression against one S3 object and receive selected records instead of downloading the entire object first.

It supports a subset of SQL over CSV, JSON and Apache Parquet inputs, with CSV or JSON output. For example, an application could request particular employee fields or filter records by department.

The single-object scope matters. A query against S3Object isn't a query across a bucket or a database of related tables.

In its July 2024 announcement, AWS closed new customer access to S3 Select, effective July 25, 2024. Existing eligible customers can continue using it. AWS also stated that it did not plan to introduce new capabilities.

The distinction is eligibility to use S3 Select, rather than simply how old an AWS account is. An older account isn't automatically evidence of existing S3 Select access.

My observed MethodNotAllowed response is consistent with these availability restrictions. However, AWS Support did not independently confirm the test account's eligibility, and the error alone does not establish the exact cause.

That qualification matters throughout the tests below.

I created a CSV with six employee records and a header row. It was 166 bytes, uncompressed, and encrypted with SSE-S3 (AES256).

The upload, metadata verification and object preview succeeded. In the query dialog, I chose CSV input, None for compression, and CSV output, then submitted the query at the start of this post.

Detail crops from the same testing screenshot: the CSV settings and failure notification.

Buckets displayed the failure rather than query results. Being able to upload and preview the object established that those workflows worked; it did not establish that S3 Select was available.

SSE-S3 also wasn't inherently an incompatible choice: the SelectObjectContent API documentation supports server-side encrypted objects, including SSE-S3.

I next used AWS CLI to separate the application interface from the underlying service operation. This is the equivalent command with generic resource names:

FileHeaderInfo: USE tells S3 Select to treat the first CSV line as a header. The operation and serialization options are documented in the AWS CLI reference.

The independent CLI attempt returned the same error. Private resource and profile details are covered.

This was the strongest evidence in the investigation: the failure was reproducible without Buckets. It narrowed the investigation, while leaving the exact AWS-side reason unresolved.

The next scenario was intended to check SQL error handling. I deliberately submitted:

I received the same MethodNotAllowed response. Correcting the expression and retrying didn't change the outcome.

Invalid SQL produced the same service response as the corrected query.

The editor remained responsive and let me retry. But I did not verify how Buckets presents an actual SQL syntax error: no parser-specific response was observed. The test also cannot reveal AWS's internal order of request validation.

I then created an uncompressed JSON Lines file with five employee records, totaling 314 bytes. Buckets identified it as JSON. With JSON input and CSV output selected, I submitted:

The JSON query also returned MethodNotAllowed; no matching records were returned.

The expression was intended to select names and departments for Engineering employees. That describes the query's intention, rather than an observed result. AWS rejected this request with the same error as the CSV request.

During the later JSON testing, v2.8.9 gave a more useful explanation of the service restriction. It discussed possible S3 Select availability issues and retained the original AWS error code, response and request identifiers.

The explanation was careful about uncertainty: possible causes included account eligibility and unsupported resource types. The AWS API reference confirms that directory buckets and S3 on Outposts do not support this operation. Those are documented possibilities, not confirmed findings about my test environment.

The expanded response is visible in the BZIP2 screenshot below, with request identifiers covered. It helped explain why repeatedly rewriting the SQL might not resolve this particular failure.

Buckets offered None, GZIP and BZIP2 in its compression dropdown. I could select GZIP and BZIP2 successfully.

GZIP was selectable. Selecting an option does not compress the stored object.

I reused the existing uncompressed JSON object and selected BZIP2. No compression-specific mismatch warning appeared before submission. When I ran the query, AWS returned MethodNotAllowed again.

BZIP2 selected for an uncompressed object. The displayed explanation concerns S3 Select availability, not a detected compression mismatch.

The dropdown worked as a UI control. Actual AWS decompression and compression-mismatch handling were not verified. I cannot claim that Buckets detected the mismatch.

AWS's format requirements distinguish these cases:

S3 Select also has a 256 KB SQL-expression limit and a 1 MB input/result-record limit. These constraints are separate from service eligibility. Proper compression testing would need working S3 Select access and correctly compressed fixtures as well as mismatch cases.

These tests made the responsibility boundary clearer:

The CLI reproduction supports treating this observed failure as an AWS service response rather than assuming a defect in the Buckets SQL editor. It doesn't certify every part of the application: successful execution, syntax-error presentation and compression processing still need verification in an eligible environment.

For SQL over a dataset spanning many S3 objects, Amazon Athena is a more suitable direction. It is a serverless analytics service that queries S3 data using SQL, with broader analytical capabilities than S3 Select's single-object API.

In a common setup, the AWS Glue Data Catalog stores table metadata: column names and types, data locations, format information and partitions. A crawler can discover that metadata, or it can be defined manually. A crawler is optional; see the Glue catalog documentation.

Illustrative architecture. Glue supplies metadata; it is not an intermediate store for the source records.

After defining a suitable employee table, the equivalent analytical query could be:

This is an illustrative Athena query, not something successfully executed during these S3 Select tests. Moving to Athena requires table definitions, permissions and result configuration; it isn't just changing an API name.

With customer-managed results, Athena writes to an S3 location selected by the client or workgroup. An enforced workgroup setting takes precedence. For API calls, StartQueryExecution.OutputLocation supplies the client-side location; a console setting alone does not configure API calls. See result-location settings.

Alternatively, a workgroup can enable Athena managed query results. This removes the need to supply your own results bucket. Results are retained for 24 hours at no additional managed-results storage cost, then automatically deleted. This mode does not support query-result reuse.

AWS currently illustrates on-demand Athena SQL pricing at US$5 per TB scanned on its pricing page. Check the applicable Region and current rate before estimating a workload. Billing concerns data scanned, rather than just the rows returned. AWS documents rounding to the next MB and a 10 MB minimum per query in its cost-model guidance.

Other models include SQL Capacity Reservations, billed for compute capacity, and Apache Spark, billed for compute use. S3 storage, requests and applicable transfer charges remain relevant, including customer-managed results. Glue catalog usage can incur charges beyond its free allowance; optional crawlers and ETL jobs are billed separately under Glue pricing.

For larger datasets, partitioning, compression and columnar formats such as Parquet can reduce scans. Workgroup scan controls can help constrain query usage.

Athena still has service quotas, including regional concurrency and query timeouts, and a 262,144-byte query-string limit. Its SQL limitations include a 32 MB row/column size ceiling.

For my 166-byte CSV or 314-byte JSON fixture, I would usually download the object and filter it with Python, pandas or DuckDB. That avoids catalog setup for a tiny one-off task, though S3 requests, transfer and local resources still matter. Athena becomes more compelling when the dataset and repeated analytical work justify that setup.

No scenario produced successful S3 Select query results. The useful findings were the independent CLI reproduction, the working input controls, and the clearer explanation in v2.8.9.

The lesson I will carry forward is to reproduce an unexpected service error outside the application before deciding which layer needs fixing. Here, that prevented a blocked execution test from becoming an unsupported claim about SQL or compression behavior.

My next article will follow the Glue, Athena and S3 Tables workflow: defining queryable data, running SQL, inspecting result storage and understanding the costs. Those are the next steps to test, rather than outcomes established here.

Confluence Page Approval Workflow, Step by Step

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Edited on Nov 6 • Originally published at leanzero.net

If you've ever been asked to "just add an approval step" to a Confluence space, you've probably found the 2019 Community thread that comes up first. In June 2019 someone asked for a "multi-tier (each tier has three people) document approval workflow," and the first reply was "there's no approval workflow out of the box." That stayed true for seven more years. On Atlassian's public tracker, the best-known request, CONFCLOUD-4153, had been closed as Won't Fix back in 2011. It has 94 votes today.

Now it's changed: Confluence Cloud has a native page approval workflow. On 10 July 2026 Atlassian put native Approvals in Confluence into open beta for Premium and Enterprise. On 5 November we set it up on our test site with two test accounts, Elzi and Theo, as approvers. When Elzi approved, the page said 1/2 and stayed In review. It only turned Approved when Theo approved too, because every approver you name has to approve.

That all-or-nothing rule is the main limit, and it's the one the 2019 thread would hit: there's no "any one of these three" or "two of three." Approvers can't be groups either. Automation can start an approval but can't react to one. And on Standard you still only have content status, the coloured label next to the page byline (automation calls it page status), which any editor can change. The one exception is Verified, a built-in status that only page owners and admins can set.

So this tutorial does both. First the native setup, step by step, with what we saw on screen. Then the Standard version with content status and automation. At the end there's a multi-approver setup with our own app, Sentinel Vault, for teams that need any-one or minimum-N rules.

When you're done you'll have a space where pages go to named approvers, a record of who approved and when, and a clear idea of where the native feature stops.

Approvals work on pages. The doc says they're "supported on pages, but not on live docs or blog posts." Live docs are Confluence's always-published pages, which have no drafts. If your team writes policies as live docs, keep reading, because live docs also block the publish gate in Step 2.

Then there's the status of the feature itself. The announcement title still says "Open Beta," and we couldn't find a general-availability notice anywhere (checked 5 November 2026). The Start approval menu item on our site carries a Beta tag too. So expect it to keep changing for a while.

Approvals start switched off, and two people turn them on. The announcement puts it like this: "A product admin enables it for your site under Configuration → New features, then space admins turn it on per space under Space settings → Status and approvals."

There's a shortcut to the first one. Even before the feature is on, a page's Status menu shows Start approval. On our site, selecting it opened a small promo whose Enable in Settings button went straight to New features.

If the approval settings don't show up, check the mode. The doc warns that "Choosing Enable statuses only or Disabled hides the approval settings." And if turning the feature on fails with "Something went wrong," see If it didn't work at the end.

The same Status and approvals screen has three switches under the approval statuses. They decide who approves, how long an approval lasts and whether a page has to wait for one.

Required approvers is a good fit for a policy space where the same people always sign. The screen calls it "Limit all approvals in this space to specific approvers (maximum of 5)," and the announcement adds that "requesters can't add or remove reviewers" while it's on.

Expiration covers yearly re-reviews. The screen says "We'll notify the page owner and requester before the expiration date."

The publish gate needs a closer look. The screen describes it as "Require approval before publishing or updating any page." That wording matches the October update Atlassian added to the beta announcement, which says the gate now covers "every publish meaning it applies to existing page edits." The support doc, published in July, still says "new pages must have approval before publishing." So the doc lags the product here. If live docs are on, the Manage live docs link on the same screen is where you turn them off. Our sources don't say what that does to the live docs you already have, so check that before you switch.

This is the one native setting our run didn't reach. We left live docs on in our test space, so the toggle stayed greyed out and we didn't test the gate itself. Anything this article says about the gate comes from Atlassian's description.

You start an approval from the page's status menu, the one you already use for a content status. Our test page was at Ready for review before we started. Start approval sits at the bottom with a Beta tag.

The panel asks for approvers, a message (up to 250 characters) and an optional due date. Approvers get reminders and "can still respond after this date," as the panel puts it.

Approvers are people only. We typed the group names confluence-users and site-admins and got "No options." In the same field, Elzi came up on the first try. So did Theo. You can't approve your own page, either: adding ourselves gave "You can't add yourself as an approver."

Elzi approved first. The banner moved to 1/2, and the page stayed In review.

When Theo approved too, the byline turned Approved. To confirm it worked, check the banner under the byline. You should see this:

The space settings define it the same way: "Approved: All approvers have approved the page."

One Request changes is enough to hold the page back: per the space settings, the page moves to Changes requested ("At least one approver requested changes to the page"). The doc says approvers "can edit your response until the approval is complete."

Confluence keeps the history in the page's activity log. Per the doc, it "shows past approval cycles, including who requested approval, who approved the page or requested changes, any comments, and when each decision happened." If an auditor asks who approved a page, that log is the answer. Open it once on a test page to see what it keeps.

Here's what happens with the publish gate off, as it was in our space. We did this as the person who'd requested the approval.

On our page the byline went back to Ready for review. Nobody was asked to approve again.

So with the gate off, an edit doesn't sneak past an approval. It removes it. After any edit, check the byline: if it no longer says Approved, start a new approval as in Step 3. Only the requester's edit is covered here; nobody else edited this page. With the gate on, Atlassian's October update says "every subsequent edit will also require a fresh approval before it can go live." Step 2 explains why that part is Atlassian's word.

None of this is hidden. Atlassian's own tracker lists the gaps.

CONFCLOUD-85363 was opened on 28 October 2026 and has 12 votes. It says "Native Confluence Cloud page approvals currently require approval from every designated approver." It also says there's no setting for "approval by any one of the designated approvers (1-of-N), or a minimum threshold/quorum, such as 2 of 3 approvers."

The workaround field reads "Currently there is no known workaround." Our 1/2 test in Step 3 matches it.

So the 2019 ask, three people per tier, works natively only if all three sign. If one of them is on holiday for two weeks, the page waits two weeks. Nobody can stand in for them. Stages and backup approvers aren't there either. We looked in the panel and in the space settings.

In the automation flow builder, a trigger search for "approv" returned "No triggers match your search." The same search under actions found one Confluence action, Start document approval.

CONFCLOUD-85157 (3 votes) says the same thing from Atlassian's side: "there is no dedicated Confluence Automation trigger that fires when a page approval status changes." It adds that the Page status changed trigger "does not detect Confluence Approval lifecycle events." So a rule like "when a policy is approved, copy it to the published space" can't be built on approvals yet.

CONFCLOUD-85194 (5 votes) says "No approval data is available via any public Confluence REST API (v1 or v2)." So a Marketplace app or your own REST script can't see who approved a page. Atlassian's own tools are the exception. Per the September update in the same announcement, its MCP server (the connector AI assistants use to reach Atlassian), the Teamwork Graph CLI and Rovo can start and track approvals. Those weren't part of our run.

During the beta it's Premium and Enterprise. When people asked about Standard, the team answered "We hear you on Standard," with no date.

The three tickets are public, so you can check them yourself before you plan around a gap. This loop asks Atlassian's public Jira for each ticket's status and vote count (you need curl and jq):

You should see one line per ticket. On 5 November 2026 it printed:

If a ticket reads anything other than Gathering Interest when you run it, that gap may have closed, so check the ticket before you buy anything.

If you're on Standard, content status plus Confluence Automation is what you've got. A status looks a lot like an approval step. In practice it works like a label you stick on the page.

Statuses are "a combination of a colored indicator (purple, blue, red, yellow, or green) and a label (up to 20 characters)." The defaults are Rough draft, In progress and Ready for review, and a space can have "up to five."

The catch is one line in the content status doc: "anyone with edit permissions can set the content status." Nothing in that doc says a status restricts anything.

Verified is the exception. Atlassian limits it to "page owners or space/site admins," which still lets an author verify their own page.

In August 2025 Atlassian's Customer Success team published a recipe that changes an unauthorised Verified back, Restrict who can Approve Pages in Confluence. Here it is as steps:

Optional: add a Restrict page action to lock edits once a page is approved. The actions doc says it changes "a page's view and edit permissions."

We opened the Page status changed trigger in the flow builder on our site but didn't save a rule. So try yours on a scratch page before you trust it. Two old bug reports on this trigger, CONFCLOUD-79255 and AUTO-872, were closed as timed out.

It has gaps, though. Even when it works, the page says Verified for a moment before the rule changes it back. A status also belongs to the page, so it doesn't change when the text does. A vendor's reply in a 2026 Community thread put it as "the status sits on the page itself, not on a specific version." And one status change is one person, so "two of these three" means counting you'd build yourself.

So is it good enough? I think it's fine for a team wiki where "approved" means "my lead looked at it." For a policy an auditor will ask about, I wouldn't lean on it.

Sentinel Vault is our own app, so read this part knowing that. Sentinel Vault is a Forge app (Forge is Atlassian's platform for cloud apps) that Mihai Perdum built at LeanZero. 8.26.0 is the version on the Marketplace today. We ran the steps below on our test site with the development build, which also reports 8.26.0.

The workflow is off in each space until a space admin turns it on. It's called Document Approval and has four states: Draft, In Review, Approved and Needs re-review.

Space admins can approve without being listed. That's on by default, and in a strict policy space you may want to switch it off. With "any one" their approval completes the request, and with "at least N" it counts toward N. The person who asked never counts on their own request.

This choice applies when someone who isn't an approver or a space admin edits an Approved page. The default is Move it back to an earlier state: their edit stays and the page goes back for a new review, to Draft unless you pick another state.

The stricter option is Revert to the approved version. Their edit is discarded and the approved version comes back as a new page version with the message "(Sentinel Vault reverted unapproved change to the approved version)." Their text stays in the page history.

A Forge app can't veto a Confluence save, so Sentinel Vault catches the edit after it's saved. It checks when Confluence sends the page event, when someone opens the page, and in an hourly sweep. Page events can arrive late: on 15 September 2026 we measured them reaching the app 9 to 10 minutes after the edit, on our test site, on one day. So if nobody opens the page, the unapproved text can sit there for minutes.

Atlassian says native Approvals with the publish gate on stop the publish itself (see Step 2), which is a real point in their favour.

Both choices depend on one site-wide Sentinel Vault setting, Protect Sealed Attachments in Page Body, which is on by default. Despite the name, it switches the whole page-body guard, and Approved-page enforcement runs on that guard. If a site admin turns it off, nothing is moved back or reverted. The Workflow tab and page banner then say Approved pages aren't protected.

Sentinel Vault adds a ribbon to each page under workflow. It shows the page's state, and its state chip is the button you use to move the page between states. My work is the app's page listing each person's pending approvals.

To start, open the Sentinel Vault item in the page's byline and select Start workflow on this page. The page starts in Draft.

Next, use the state chip to move the page to In Review. Then use it again and choose Request approval → Approved. The ribbon shows 0 of 2. Elzi's My work page showed "1 waiting on you."

Each approver opens the request from the ribbon, can add a reason (it's optional) and selects Approve. After the last approval the ribbon reads Approved v1. Elzi approved with "Meal limit checked with Finance." Theo approved with "Booking rules match the travel desk contract."

Then Iris, who is neither an approver nor an admin, edited the page. About 30 seconds after she published, the ribbon read Draft. We didn't record which check caught it; opening the page runs one. The Activity tab had the whole story.

A space admin can filter that tab and export it as CSV. History is kept unless a site admin turns on Delete old history. Then entries older than 730 days (the default) are deleted weekly.

Approved pages also get a re-review date: 150 days by default, and we set 180. The hourly sweep moves a page to Needs re-review at the first run after that date, usually within the hour. On sites with more than 2,000 pages under workflow it can take longer. Native approval expiration covers the same need, so pick whichever tool you're already on.

You can watch the whole flow in Mihai's two-minute video, Confluence Page Approval: Draft to Approved, Enforced.

Require a signed decision makes each approver type the 6-digit code from their authenticator app when they approve or deny. They set it up once on My work, under Your approval signature. Codes are single-use, and five wrong codes lock that person's signing for 15 minutes. We left it off in this run.

What it proves is that the approver had a device enrolled with the app. It's one factor, and it doesn't make a decision an electronic signature in the regulatory sense. 21 CFR 11.200 asks for "at least two distinct identification components," and we don't claim Part 11 or any other certification.

It doesn't block the save. It doesn't read or write native Confluence Approvals, so pick one per space. Approval covers the page text. To hold a file, seal it (Sentinel Vault's lock for a single attachment); a sealed file on an Approved page stays sealed until the approval ends. There's a separate tutorial on locking attachments in Confluence Cloud. This run used "all approvers" and the move-back option. We didn't test Revert, "at least N," signed decisions, or Sentinel Vault and native Approvals on the same page. The listing says it's licensed under Apache 2.0 and its 10-user tier costs nothing.

Other apps cover approvals too. Appfire's Page Approval and Comala Document Approval, AppFox's Approvals for Confluence, Approval Path, Aura Apps' AURA Workflow & Approval, Approval Seal and Sealed are all on the Marketplace (checked 5 November 2026). I haven't tried them, so I won't rank them.

Before you roll a route out to a real space, run it once on a scratch page with two other accounts. This is what you should see:

Here are the messages you're most likely to hit, with the fix for each.

The parts our run didn't reach are named where they apply: the publish gate in Step 2, other editors in Step 4, the automation rule in Step 5 and the Sentinel Vault options in Step 6. Try those on a scratch space before you rely on them.

If I were picking today, I'd probably go like this. On Premium or Enterprise, with a fixed group of people who will all sign, use native Approvals with Required approvers and the publish gate on, with live docs off in that space. The gate is the one setting our run didn't reach (Step 2), so that part rests on Atlassian's description. On Standard, where approval means "my lead looked at it," page status plus the automation recipe in Step 5 will do. And if you need any one of three, two of three, group approvers, or a code on each sign-off, you'll need an app. Sentinel Vault is ours, and the product page has the details.

If you run approvals in Confluence today, I'd love to hear which of these you picked and why. And if change requests in Jira are your other headache, our Jira CAB approval tutorial covers that side.

Originally published on leanzero.net. More Atlassian, Forge and local-AI write-ups at leanzero.net/blog, and if you're planning a migration or a Forge app, that's what we do: leanzero.net/services.

My Support-FAQ Bot Made Up 22 Answers It Didn't Know. Grounding It in n8n Dropped That to 0

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A support bot that says "I don't know" is annoying. A support bot that confidently says "No, we don't have a phone number" when nobody ever wrote that down is a liability.

So I tested mine. I gave a small support-FAQ workflow a 6-entry knowledge base and asked it 20 questions: 10 the knowledge base answers, and 10 it doesn't. Then I asked every question three times, to a "raw" version and a "grounded" version.

Setup: n8n 1.123.84 self-hosted, Qwen 1.8B (Q4_K_M) in Ollama on CPU through its OpenAI-compatible /v1 endpoint, temperature 0.2, response_format: json_object. Two workflows: Webhook, Config, Build Prompt, LLM, a Code node, Respond. 20 questions x 3 rounds x 2 versions = 120 runs. Average latency 1.4 s.

The knowledge base is tiny on purpose: refunds (14 days, one email address), pricing (Starter $19, Pro $49, no annual billing), support hours, a 7-day trial with no card, cancellation, and CSV export for Pro. The 10 questions it can't answer include a student discount, a phone number, a Slack integration, GDPR, adding team members, an iPhone app, an uptime SLA, GST invoices, password reset, and a refund after 30 days.

The prompt is what most tutorials ship: "You are a friendly support agent. Use the knowledge base to help the customer. Return JSON {answer, escalate}. Set escalate=true only if you cannot help."

On known questions it was perfect. That's exactly why it's dangerous: you test it with the questions you know the answer to, it passes, and you ship it.

I read all 29 answers it gave to questions it had no answer for. 22 of them asserted something that isn't in the knowledge base:

Some of those "no" answers might even be true. That's not the point. Nobody told the bot, and nobody checked. A customer who hears "you can't have that" from a support bot doesn't buy.

The other 7: three answered the GDPR question with the CSV export entry (off-topic, not an invention), three answered "refund after 30 days?" with "no, 14 days only" (which is what the KB implies, so I counted it as fine), and one said "not in the knowledge base" but didn't escalate.

The fix isn't a sterner prompt. My grounded prompt also says "answer ONLY from the knowledge base", and a 1.8B model still wrote answers that weren't in it. The fix is to make the model hand over evidence, and have a Code node check it.

The prompt asks for {answer, kb_id, quote, escalate}: which entry it used, and the sentence it copied from it. The Code node then decides:

Zero made-up answers. But look at the last row: it threw away 18 of 30 correct answers.

The model is bad at filling in bookkeeping fields, not at answering. It wrote kb_id: "refund" (the id is refunds), "free_trial" (the id is trial), and often kb_id: "none" while quoting the right sentence word for word. A guard that trusts the model's own labels punishes the model for typos.

It also did catch the model faking evidence. On the questions it didn't know, the "quote" was often just the answer it had made up, copied into the quote field: "Reset your password by going to Settings > Passwords and clicking on 'Forgot Password?'" The quote check failed it every time, and the customer got the fallback.

Same prompt, same model outputs. I changed the Code node to ignore kb_id and the model's escalate flag entirely and only ask: is this quote a real stretch of the knowledge base?

I replayed the 60 saved grounded outputs through it (no new model calls):

The 3 that "got through" were the 30-day refund question, answered "full refunds are only available within 14 days of purchase", a real quote from a real entry. That's the correct answer, so I'd call it a pass. The 4 wrongly escalated were the model paraphrasing the quote instead of copying it, and falling back to a human is a cheap failure.

It checks that the quote exists, not that it answers the question. A model that quotes the right entry for the wrong question will pass. The GDPR question was answered with the CSV export sentence in the raw version, and a quote check wouldn't stop that if the model quoted the export entry accurately. In my run it didn't get that far, because the model wrote its own text into the quote field, but I wouldn't rely on luck. For that you'd add a keyword overlap check between the question and the entry, or a human spot-check on the first 50 answers.

Also, with only 6 KB entries and 1.8B parameters, the results will be different on your KB and your model. The 22 isn't a rate you should expect. What carries over is the method: ask the model for evidence, and let code decide whether the evidence is real.

The support-FAQ workflows from this test aren't in the free repo yet, and the Code node above is the important part: paste it after any LLM node that answers from a knowledge base. The free workflows in arkintof/n8n-ai-agent-workflows right now are the lead qualifier and the review reply bot, and the idea carries over to both: let the model propose, let code decide.

What's the most confident wrong answer your support bot has ever given a customer? Did you find out from a log, or from the customer?

Written with AI assistance; workflow built and tested by me.

Two Futures of AI-Powered BI: Dashboard + AI Agent vs. Agent-First

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at blog.luca-liu.com

In my previous article, I explained how AI Agents could change Business Intelligence (BI) architecture. Instead of opening dashboards, business users could directly ask AI agents to analyze data.

After reviewing the BI projects that I made in the real world, I started to see two possible approaches for the future of BI :

Dashboard + AI Agent, and Agent-First BI.

I think the choice between them depends on the scope of the data, the number of users, and how predictable their questions are.

Let me start with a project I worked on. I developed a procurement analytics solution called SpendCube using Azure Databricks, Azure SQL Database, and Power BI.

The project had a clearly defined business scope. It was designed only for the procurement team, and all the KPIs were related to procurement activities.

Some of these KPIs had complex business rules. I needed to discuss requirements with business owners, agree on calculation methods, and validate the results.

However, the purpose of the system was clear: help the procurement team monitor and analyze its purchasing activities.

For this kind of project, I believe a dashboard combined with an AI agent would be a very practical solution.

The dashboard provides a clear overview of important KPIs. Users can quickly check purchasing performance, compare different periods, and identify changes.

At the same time, an AI agent could provide more flexibility. AI agent could investigate the data, identify possible reasons, and create additional visualizations.

For companies that already have Power BI dashboards, the next step could simply be adding an AI agent instead of rebuilding the entire BI system.

Power BI Copilot is a good example of this approach. Organizations with the required Premium or Fabric capacity can use Copilot to let users explore their data through natural language.

The key challenge is making sure the AI agent and the dashboard return the same results. If a user asks the AI agent for a KPI that already exists in the dashboard, both should provide the same value. To achieve this, the AI agent should use the existing semantic model, including its approved DAX measures and business rules, rather than creating its own calculations.

Now let's consider a very different situation.

In my previous article, I discussed BMW's Mobile Data Recorder project.

According to Microsoft's case study, BMW operates around 3,500 development vehicles, and its system collects more than 10,000 signals per vehicle.

Imagine being an engineer working with this enormous dataset.

One engineer wants to analyze braking behavior under certain conditions. Another wants to investigate battery performance. A third wants to understand unusual sensor readings.

Different engineers work on different problems, and their questions can change every day.

Unlike my SpendCube project, this isn't a situation where a small group of users mainly monitors a fixed set of KPIs.

It would be extremely difficult to predict all possible questions and build dashboards for them in advance.

Even if hundreds of dashboards existed, engineers might still need new queries and visualizations to answer their specific questions.

For this kind of environment, I believe an agent-first approach would make more sense.

Instead of opening a dashboard and looking for the right chart, engineers could directly describe what they want to investigate.

The AI agent could identify relevant data, generate queries, analyze the results, and create visualizations when needed.

In fact, BMW has already introduced elements of this approach. Its MDR copilot uses natural language to generate KQL queries and can also use knowledge collected from expert interviews.

Instead of building dashboards for questions we can predict, we could build AI agents that help users answer questions we cannot predict.

The choice is not mainly about company size or data volume, but how clearly the business scope is defined and how predictable users' questions are.

For well-defined business requirements, a dashboard combined with an AI agent is often a practical solution.

For complex, open-ended data exploration, an agent-first approach may offer more flexibility.

Both approaches can also exist within the same company.

I believe AI agents will become an important part of Business Intelligence, but not every BI system needs to become agent-first.

The future of BI is not about replacing dashboards, but choosing the right interface for different analytical needs.

For data professionals, this also means a shift in focus: from building individual dashboards to creating trusted, reusable data foundations that support both dashboards and AI agents.

Thanks for reading Data in the Age of AI. Follow along for more thoughts on data, AI, and Business Intelligence, or connect with me on LinkedIn.

Thank you for taking the time to explore data-related insights with me. I appreciate your engagement.

Calibration‑free top‑k MoE selector saves tuning

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

MoE decoding now runs at full speed without any offline threshold tuning. The new top‑k channel selector discards calibrated thresholds entirely, yet keeps the same accuracy and latency advantages that experts have come to expect.

Previously, practitioners limited routed‑expert reads by calibrating a score threshold on held‑out data; Prox + thresholds were the de facto recipe for interactive decoding at batch size one. This calibration step was required to hit a target read budget but introduced variability across inputs and even caused repetition loops in models such as Gemma‑4.

Top‑k selection removes the need for any calibrated threshold. “Second, top‑ keeps each token’s highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data.” [1] By simply retaining each token’s k highest‑scoring channels, the selector selects a consistent number of channels per token without requiring an external tuning phase.

Across a wide range of benchmarks, the calibration‑free top‑k version matches or exceeds its threshold counterpart. “At the calibrated budget, a selector’s top‑ version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks.” [1] The superiority holds for both classification‑style and generative workloads.

The accompanying reader preserves almost full model performance while slashing weight reads. “Across three post‑trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full‑model accuracy at 17.8% of routed‑expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90 faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69 at larger batches on the same three models.” [1] The ternary sketch plus low‑rank recovery map delivers exact top‑k channels directly from BF16 weights.

The paper’s evaluation is limited to three post‑trained MoE checkpoints and relies on Triton kernels, leaving open whether the same gains hold for training‑time experts or alternative backends. Moreover, the ternary sketch introduces a small compute overhead that could dominate in ultra‑low‑latency settings. This suggests further work is needed to confirm calibration‑free selection across broader model families and hardware stacks.

If the results generalize, deployment pipelines can drop per‑model threshold calibration entirely and adopt the provided vLLM‑compatible reader as the default inference path for MoE models. The simplification cuts CI/CD latency, reduces engineering effort, and makes MoE acceleration a plug‑and‑play component.

‘Worst days in history’ – Trump’s stock-market reaction meets crypto’s $900B slide

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Bloodbath is the perfect word to describe the post-election market action.

Despite the markets trading on the assumption that Democrats will win, with odds hitting a high of 99%, crypto has suffered a severe sell-off. However, the downside may not be over yet. The total crypto market cap has now slipped back to mid-September levels, marking five straight weeks of losses and wiping out nearly $900 billion in market value.

The result? A textbook liquidity sweep. According to Coinglass, nearly $1 billion in positions were liquidated on the 5th of September, marking the largest single-day wipeout since the $2 billion short squeeze in mid-August. However, unlike that event, nearly 80% of the liquidations came from longs, showing that bullish traders were heavily caught offside as the market moved lower.

That said, the sell-off wasn’t just “crypto-specific.”

As the chart above shows, the Nasdaq 100 suffered its largest one-day loss since the pandemic, while the S&P500 lost $3 trillion in value. This comes just 24 hours after the Dow surged 1,600 points after Democrats swept the midterms. This indicates that there has been an abrupt change in sentiment post-election.

Notably, the U.S. President Donald Trump took to Truth Social to say that it is one of the stock market’s “worst days in history.” The crypto wipeout seems to be part of a wider correction in the market, with post-election volatility adding to the selling pressure. However, the move may not be just another election-driven pullback. The latest market data suggests there could be more going on beneath the surface.

The market FUD seems to be less about the election itself and more about President Trump.

From a technical standpoint, this shift in sentiment makes sense. Crypto initially saw a strong boost from optimism around his election, but that momentum has since faded. However, the momentum has died out. Bitcoin [BTC] is currently down almost 30% from its peak of $126k despite progress made in regulation with the GENIUS Act, greater tokenization and increased adoption in stablecoins into traditional finance.

To add to the pressure, there are now reports that Trump Media sold a total of $343 million worth of Bitcoin. This adds to concerns regarding institutional selling and has also created more FUD around Trump, further weakening the bullish sentiment that initially drove crypto’s post-election rally.

Against this backdrop, crypto’s wipeout could be more than just a reaction to broader market weakness.

Besides the traditional markets digesting how the election will affect the AI space, there are further pressures on the crypto space. On top of reports that Trump Media is selling off its Bitcoin, there continue to be outflows from ETFs. In essence, crypto is facing its own market-specific headwinds.

In turn, crypto could be entering a more severe capitulation phase now that prices have retreated back to mid-September levels.

Crypto has lost nearly $900 billion in market value, with Bitcoin down almost 30% from its $126k peak.

Trump Media’s reported Bitcoin sales and continued ETF outflows are adding to selling pressure, raising fears of deeper capitulation.

You Should Update to iOS 27.2 Now to Patch Hundreds of Bugs

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems

The update fixes some bugs that could expose your sensitive iPhone data or worse.

Apple released iOS 27.2 on Monday, Oct. 26, just a few days after the launch of the iPhone Duo. While iOS 27.2 brings Siri AI to more languages and updates a few other features, it also patches 141 bugs and security issues, including some that could allow a third party to access data or run commands on your iPhone or leave your device inoperable.

You can download iOS 27.2 now by going to Settings > General > Software Update > Update Now and following the prompts on your screen.

According to Apple’s release notes, iOS 27.2 patches dozens of issues across your iPhone. Some of these issues can cause some serious problems for you and your device.

Nine of these bugs address issues that could lead to kernel corruption on your device. The kernel is the core of your device’s operating system, and kernel corruption can cause your device to freeze or crash. The blue screen of death, for example, could be caused by kernel corruption.

Apple also wrote that seven of these bugs could lead to arbitrary code execution. That means the bug could allow a third party to steal your data or use your device for other nefarious purposes.

The update addresses a lot of other bugs, including one that you might run into if you plug a malicious USB into your device. Apple didn’t write that any of these bugs were being actively exploited, but you should still update to iOS 27.2 now to give yourself the most protection.

For as many bugs as 27.2 fixes, it’s actually a slight decrease from earlier updates. Last month’s iOS 27 update patched even more bugs at 157. The number of bug fixes in both iOS updates are unusual based on data over the past few years. In iOS 26, the most bug fixes included in an update was 88 with iOS 26.6. The update with the next highest number of patches was iOS 18.4 with 75.

Apple didn’t say why there’s been an uptick in the number of bug fixes in iOS updates. Artificial intelligence has been used recently to find and address huge numbers of software bugs; however, that doesn’t appear to be the cause behind the surge in iOS 27.2 or iOS 27. Apple usually notes when a bug is found with the help of AI, yet OpenAI is mentioned four times in iOS 27.2’s release notes, and Claude’s Anthropic is only mentioned thrice.

Apple didn’t immediately respond to my request to comment.

For more iOS news, here’s what you should know about iOS 27.2 and iOS 27.1. You can also check out our iOS 27 cheat sheet.

Melissa Joan Hart Explains It All: Hot Flashes, Menopause, and Turning 50

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Actor Melissa Joan Hart has a vivid memory of her mother standing in front of their open freezer to cool off. As a child, she didn’t understand why. Now, as a 50-year-old woman, Hart knows that her mom was experiencing hot flashes.

“[She’s] only 20 years older than me, so maybe I missed the part where she was going through [menopause], but there was no conversation about it,” Hart told Healthline. “I feel like it’s just becoming a hot topic with women, where…the taboo is going away from it now.”

Her own perimenopausal symptoms started with night sweats.

“[It] was really jarring, and the first few times, you’re not really sure what that was…do I need to turn the air down or what’s going on here?” Hart said.

Over time, she learned that she was experiencing moderate to severe hot flashes, which affect up to 80% of women during the menopause transition and can persist for 7 to 10 years.

Doctors categorize hot flashes as mild, moderate, or severe, said Diana Bitner, MD, OB-GYN, at Corewell Health.

“[So] if they’re mild, it’s a sense of warmth (maybe in the chest, or the neck, or the face). A moderate hot flash is where there’s associated sweating, and a severe hot flash is something that stops you in your tracks, wakes you up from the middle of the night,” Bitner told Healthline.

Hart has mostly experienced hot flashes while home or at her three sons’ activities, and they have yet to interfere with her work. However, according to research, one in three women report moderate to severe challenges in managing their work due to symptoms related to menopause.

“It’s just so sad that about 10% of women leave the workforce due to menopause,” said Bitner. “And so it can really affect our ability to perform at these key years of not only institutional memory, but also of earning power, and it can really affect the whole community.”

Bitner said women should feel empowered to bring up their menopausal symptoms or questions with their doctor.

“[This] is something that was taboo for so long…women didn’t really talk about this, whether it was because of embarrassment, or [they] weren’t sure if anyone was experiencing it,” she said. “[There’s] no reason, in this day and age, we need to suffer through it…it’s something that there are treatment options for.”

In fact, Bitner said providing specific details about their symptoms, such as how many mild, moderate, or severe hot flashes they’ve experienced over the past week, can help doctors better assess patients’ needs.

Hart plans to discuss treatment options for her hot flashes with her doctor soon. In the last few years, she said she has put emphasis on her midlife health.

“I just was sick of waking up with aches and pains in general [thinking], ‘is this the rest of my life?’” she said.

She started listening to self-help books in the car while driving her kids around and found ways to put herself first.

“My kids are a little bit older, so I kind of have that freedom to be able to do that,” she said. “[But] along with getting older, comes the menopause, comes that different time in life. And I think it’s a good time to reevaluate [how] our bodies can’t handle what [they] used to handle, and let’s figure it out.”

Hart has partnered with pharmaceutical company Bayer on its “Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash” campaign, which encourages women to discuss menopause symptoms with their doctors and promotes Lynkuet, a hormone-free prescription medication for moderate to severe hot flashes associated with menopause.

“It’s called ‘Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash’ campaign because it shouldn’t, because we should be able to push through, and this should be a time in our lives when our kids are getting older, and we’re able to kind of reinvent ourselves,” said Hart. “And I think it’s an exciting time, and it shouldn’t be something that we’re afraid of.”

She is embracing speaking openly about midlife health.

“I hit 50 this year, and it was, like, a huge milestone, and I think that’s something to be proud of, and not something to shy away from,” she said.

Hart also hopes to challenge the longstanding barriers women face in Hollywood as they age, including fewer opportunities for leading roles.

“[I] think we’re seeing that the generation before, and my generation, it feels like we’re pushing those boundaries…and we’re saying, you know, that women at this age have a lot to give, have a lot of information to share, have a lot of stories to tell and experiences and adventures still ahead of us, and, you know, we’re not ready to stop,” Hart said.

In everyday life, Bitner said that while some patients avoid talking about midlife issues like menopause with their doctors, she is seeing more patients who are confident being forthcoming.

As more women talk openly about menopause, she hopes to include men in the conversation, too. In fact, she invites her patients to bring their partners to appointments, so they can gain an understanding of menopause and the experiences their partners face, such as hot flashes, sleep issues, vaginal dryness, and discomfort or pain during intercourse.

“[Often], there’s, you know, comments that come up [from men who] say, ‘How do I talk about this without getting in trouble?’” said Bitner. “[We] want everyone to join this conversation…it doesn’t have to be just her issue because we know it’s not.”

Political Campaigns Spent a Near-Record $331 Per Voter, But Waste Forces Rethink

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Where are the most expensive media markets in the U.S.? At least for political campaigns in 2026, they were Maine, Alaska, and Iowa.

These thinly populated battleground states saw spending of up to $331 per registered voter for the 2026 election cycle, according to data from AdImpact and state voter rolls.

That came as total political spending hit a record $11.9 billion across TV, digital, and audio, exceeding the 2024 presidential year’s $11.2 billion. None of that includes substantial direct mail or outdoor campaigns.

Maine saw the highest spending per voter at $331, followed by Alaska at $313 and Iowa at $252. They fell just shy of a record set by Montana in 2024, which got $335 per voter thanks to a competitive Senate seat. Competitive Senate and gubernatorial races fueled a record number of three-digit outlays per voter in 2026, including Ohio ($112) and Kansas ($102).

Spending per voter doubled and even tripled from two years ago in all five triple-digit-spending states. Republicans outspent Democrats in four of those five but lost key races in all of them, per AdImpact, with the Alaska Senate race still being counted.

That so much money failed to change outcomes could force a major rethink of 2028 strategy.

Heavy spending meant local ads spilling into national buys. By October, Senate campaign ads from Iowa, Texas, Florida, Maine, and Kansas ran on some national broadcasts of NCAA and NFL football games.

A shift to digital and connected TV spending this year made targeting theoretically easier, but didn’t guarantee reaching the right people.

Sal Tuzzeo, a Fabric Media executive based near Cleveland, said his 8-year-old son was bombarded by political ads, even though he was watching age-appropriate content on the big screen from YouTube creators TerraGreen, The Fish Guys, and Unspeakable.

“The amount of political money flowing into streaming and CTV seemed absurd,” Tuzzeo said. “The mistargeting was egregious.” He attributed it to “a broad reach land grab coupled with overt aversion to frequency capping.”

Over the past few months, nearly every ad in every pod his son watched was political, Tuzzeo said. His child could recite verbatim ads dealing with abortion, sex changes, crack cocaine, and domestic violence.

Republicans outspent Democrats by a $5.5 billion to $5 billion margin nationwide, per AdImpact. But that advantage made little difference. For example, of 87 candidates backed by Elon Musk’s America PAC, 80 lost, according to Travis Trawick, CEO of nonpartisan election tech firm FullPAC.

Too much money went into broadcast over the final five weeks, when time is most expensive and when “at least in this election, most people’s minds were already made up,” Trawick said. “Running the same ad in front of people 60 times just has diminishing returns.”

For 2028, he believes campaigns will shift spending earlier in the cycle and toward AI-driven digital and social strategies, using AI not just to optimize media but also to collect return path signals and shape creative that spawns voter engagement.

“Being able to create a conversation between a voter and a candidate or an issue, not just talking at them, and doing that at scale, is something that’s never really been possible before,” Trawick said. “Now it is.”
